Improved Scaling Laws via Weak-to-Strong Generalization in Random Feature Ridge Regression
Diyuan Wu, Lehan Chen, Theodor Misiakiewicz, Marco Mondelli

Abstract
It is increasingly common in machine learning to use learned models to label data and then employ such data to train more capable models. The phenomenon of weak-to-strong generalization exemplifies the advantage of this two-stage procedure: a strong student is trained on imperfect labels obtained from a weak teacher, and yet the strong student outperforms the weak teacher. In this paper, we show that the potential improvement is substantial, in the sense that it affects the scaling law followed by the test error. Specifically, we consider students and teachers trained via random feature ridge regression (RFRR). Our main technical contribution is to derive a deterministic equivalent for the excess test error of the student trained on labels obtained via the teacher. Via this deterministic equivalent, we then identify regimes in which the scaling law of the student improves upon that of the teacher, unveiling that the improvement can be achieved both in bias-dominated and variance-dominated settings. Strikingly, the student may attain the minimax optimal rate regardless of the scaling law of the teacher—in fact, when the test error of the teacher does not even decay with the sample size.

†
affiliationnotice:
Equal contribution

1
Introduction

Across modern machine learning pipelines, models often generate synthetic labels (or synthetic data altogether), which are then used to train other models via a cheaper—but imperfect—supervision.
This two-stage paradigm appears in knowledge distillation
(
Hinton et al. 2015
;
Panigrahi et al. 2025
)
, self-training
(
Xie et al. 2020
)
and, more recently, in proposals where weaker agents supervise stronger ones
(
Burns et al. 2023
)
. Weak-to-strong generalization (W2SG) formalizes a particularly striking version of such a proposal: a strong student (a pretrained GPT-4 model in the experiments by
Burns et al. 2023
) is fine-tuned on labels produced by a weak teacher (a pretrained GPT-2 model) and, despite the labels being imperfect, the student outperforms the teacher. This motivates the central question of
how much a strong student can improve upon its weak teacher
and, in particular, whether this improvement can happen at the level of the exponent in the
scaling law

(
Hestness et al. 2017
;
Kaplan et al. 2020
;
Hoffmann et al. 2022
)
.

On the empirical side, distillation scaling laws have characterized how student performance depends on compute allocation between teacher and student
(
Busbridge et al. 2025
)
, and related questions have been explored for scalable oversight
(
Engels et al. 2025
)
. On the theoretical side, a rich line of work has established scaling laws for linear regression
(
Maloney et al. 2022
;
Bahri et al. 2024
;
Lin et al. 2024
;
Lin et al. 2025
;
Atanasov et al. 2024
)
and simple neural networks
(
Paquette et al. 2024
;
Bordelon et al. 2024
;
Ferbach et al. 2025
;
Ren et al. 2025
;
Defilippis et al. 2024
)
. Several recent papers have also given theoretical insights into W2SG
(
Charikar et al. 2024
;
Lang et al. 2024
;
Somerstep et al. 2025
;
Yao et al. 2025
;
Moniri & Hassani 2025
;
Xue et al. 2025
)
, identifying mechanisms that allow the strong student to outperform the weak teacher, such as early stopping
(
Medvedev et al. 2025
)
, feature discrepancy
(
Dong et al. 2025
)
, or benign overfitting
(
Wu & Sahai 2024
)
. However, the only existing result relating W2SG to scaling laws is
negative
:
Ildiz et al. 2025
show that, for ridgeless linear regression, training on surrogate teacher labels can improve performance, but it is unable to improve the scaling law.

Our paper demonstrates that the interplay of regularization and over-parameterization drastically changes the picture: we establish that
improved scaling laws
are already possible in a tractable non-linear model, i.e.,
random feature ridge regression (RFRR)
. More precisely, we consider a setting where both teacher and student are trained via RFRR: first, the teacher is fit on
n_{\mathsf{t}}
labeled samples using
p_{\mathsf{t}}
random features and ridge parameter
\lambda_{\mathsf{t}}
; then, the student draws
n_{\mathsf{s}}
fresh unlabeled inputs, and it is trained purely on teacher-generated labels using
p_{\mathsf{s}}
random features and ridge parameter
\lambda_{\mathsf{s}}
.
Our contributions are summarized as follows:

•

We show a dimension-free
deterministic equivalent
, together with non-asymptotic approximation guarantees, for the excess test error incurred by the student trained with labels obtained from the teacher (Theorem
2
).

•

Then, by leveraging the deterministic equivalent, we obtain
scaling laws
for the student error under
source and capacity conditions
, i.e., power-law decay of target coefficients and covariance spectrum (Theorem
4
).

•

Finally, we compare the exponent in the scaling law of the student with the exponent in the scaling law of the teacher (the latter was derived in earlier work by
Defilippis et al. 2024
). This allows us to identify regimes in which the error of the student decays faster than that of the teacher (Corollaries
2
and
3
).

Overall, our analysis sheds light into the enabling mechanisms for weak-to-strong generalization, underscoring the importance of regularization and number of features in the model:
(i)
if the teacher is optimally tuned, then the student cannot improve the scaling law;
(ii)
if the teacher is variance-dominated (and not optimal), then the student can always improve the scaling law by properly selecting regularization and model size;
(iii)
if the teacher is bias-dominated (and not optimal), then there are still settings in which the student improves the scaling law; and
(iv)
the student may attain the minimax-optimal decay rate, no matter the scaling law of the teacher—even in scenarios where the error of the teacher does not decay to
0
as the sample size grows.

2
Related work

Weak-to-strong generalization (W2SG).

Several recent theoretical works have analyzed mechanisms leading to W2SG.
Charikar et al. 2024
focus on the misfit error incurred by the strong model on labels generated by the weaker model, and
Yao et al. 2025
extend these findings beyond the square loss to output distribution divergence.
Lang et al. 2024
suggest that W2SG occurs when the strong model cannot reproduce the teacher’s mistakes without sacrificing performance. A link to benign overfitting is provided in
Wu & Sahai 2024
, and
Xue et al. 2025
show that W2SG occurs when the parts of the weak model’s mistakes representable by the strong model are small. In high-dimensional ridgeless linear regression,
Ildiz et al. 2025
show that training on teacher labels can be better than training on clean labels, but it does not improve the exponent in the scaling law. In contrast, we show that by allowing over-parameterization and ridge regularization, the scaling law can improve. Ridge regularization is considered in

Moniri & Hassani 2025
, who show that the student can compensate for the teacher’s under-regularization.
Closer to our setting,
Medvedev et al. 2025
focus on the random feature model, but train the teacher on the population and consider students with infinitely many features, so that W2SG can only emerge as a consequence of early-stopping. W2SG arises in the variance-dominated regime in
Dong et al. 2025
, due to the fact that fine-tuning occurs in intrinsically low-dimensional spaces. In constrast, our work unveils that W2SG is possible both in variance-dominated and bias-dominated regimes. Overall, we highlight that the key distinguishing factor of this paper w.r.t. earlier work is the improvement in scaling laws and, at the technical level, the fact that we provide a dimension-free deterministic equivalent for the student error.

Scaling laws.

Bahri et al. 2024
propose a theory that explains the emergence of neural scaling laws in terms of variance-limited and resolution-limited scaling regimes. Compute/data/parameter tradeoffs are characterized by
Lin et al. 2024
;
Paquette et al. 2024
.
Lin et al. 2025
;
Ferbach et al. 2025
focus on the role of data re-use and on the choice of the optimizer, respectively; and shallow neural networks are considered in
(
Defilippis et al. 2024
;
Atanasov et al. 2024
;
Ren et al. 2025
)
.
Most closely related to our work are scaling law results for kernel and random feature methods under source/capacity conditions
(
Caponnetto & De Vito 2007
;
Rudi & Rosasco 2017
;
Cui et al. 2023
;
Defilippis et al. 2024
)
.
Our results contribute to this landscape by establishing the excess test error of a student trained on imperfect labels generated from a teacher, and by comparing student and teacher scaling laws.

Random features and deterministic equivalents.

Introduced by
Rahimi & Recht 2007
;
Balcan et al. 2006
, random feature models have been used to understand various phenomena in deep learning, including double descent
(
Mei & Montanari 2022
)
, feature learning
(
Ba et al. 2022
;
Moniri et al. 2024
)
, robustness under adversarial attacks
(
Dohmatob & Bietti 2022
;
Bombari et al. 2023
;
Hassani & Javanmard 2024
)
, spurious features and correlations
(
Bombari & Mondelli 2024
;
Bombari & Mondelli 2025
)
, and distribution shift
(
Tripuraneni et al. 2021
;
Lee et al. 2023
)
.
Dimension-free deterministic equivalents for ridge regression and random feature regression have recently enabled refined, non-asymptotic predictions of test error and scaling laws
(
Cheng & Montanari 2024
;
Defilippis et al. 2024
)
, and analogous deterministic-equivalent techniques have been developed for kernel ridge regression
(
Misiakiewicz & Saeed 2024
)
.
The deterministic equivalent we prove in this paper extends these tools to a two-stage learning pipeline, which introduces additional dependencies and cross-terms absent in the one-stage setting considered in earlier work.

3
Setting

Let
({\mathcal{X}},\mu_{x})
be an input probability space and
({\mathcal{W}},\mu_{w})
a weight probability space.
We denote by
L^{2}({\mathcal{X}},\mu_{x})
and
L^{2}({\mathcal{W}},\mu_{w})
the associated Hilbert spaces of square-integrable functions.

Random Feature (RF) model.

Let
\varphi:{\mathcal{X}}\times{\mathcal{W}}\to\mathbb{R}
be a feature map with

\varphi\in L^{2}({\mathcal{X}}\times{\mathcal{W}},\mu_{x}\otimes\mu_{w})
. The associated
random feature model
is

\hat{f}({\bm{x}};{\bm{a}})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{p}}\sum_{j=1}^{p}a_{j}\varphi({\bm{x}};{\bm{w}}_{j}),

(1)

where
{\bm{a}}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
are trainable coefficients and
{\bm{w}}_{j}\sim_{\text{i.i.d.}}\mu_{w}
are fixed random weights. Viewing
\varphi
as a compact linear operator from
L^{2}({\mathcal{X}},\mu_{x})
to
L^{2}({\mathcal{W}},\mu_{w})
, it admits a diagonalization
(
Mei et al. 2022
;
Defilippis et al. 2024
)
:

\varphi({\bm{x}};{\bm{w}})=\sum_{k=1}^{d}\xi_{k}\psi_{k}({\bm{w}})\phi_{k}({\bm{x}}),

where
|\xi_{1}|\geq|\xi_{2}|\geq\cdots
are the eigenvalues, and
\{\psi_{k}\}_{k\geq 1}
and
\{\phi_{k}\}_{k\geq 1}
are orthonormal bases of
L^{2}({\mathcal{W}},\mu_{w})
and
L^{2}({\mathcal{X}},\mu_{x})
, respectively. Define the (potentially infinite-dimensional
d=\infty
) diagonal operator

{\bm{\Sigma}}\;=\;{\rm diag}(\xi_{1}^{2},\xi_{2}^{2},\ldots),\qquad\text{with}\qquad{\rm Tr}({\bm{\Sigma}})<\infty,

which follows from
\varphi\in L^{2}({\mathcal{X}}\times{\mathcal{W}},\mu_{x}\otimes\mu_{w})
. It is convenient to rewrite the feature map in inner-product form

\varphi({\bm{x}};{\bm{w}})=\langle\bm{f},{\bm{g}}\rangle,

(2)

where
\bm{f}:=\bm{f}({\bm{w}})
and
{\bm{g}}:={\bm{g}}({\bm{x}})
with

\bm{f}({\bm{w}})=(\xi_{k}\psi_{k}({\bm{w}}))_{k\geq 1},\quad{\bm{g}}({\bm{x}})=(\phi_{k}({\bm{x}}))_{k\geq 1}.

Under
{\bm{w}}\sim\mu_{w}
and
{\bm{x}}\sim\mu_{x}
, the random vectors
\bm{f}
and
\tilde{\bm{g}}:={\bm{\Sigma}}^{1/2}{\bm{g}}
are elements of
\ell_{2}
(the space of squared-summable sequences), with covariance
{\bm{\Sigma}}
. The RF model (
1
) becomes

\hat{f}({\bm{x}};{\bm{a}})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{p}}{\bm{a}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\bm{F}}{\bm{g}},

where
{\bm{F}}:=[\bm{f}_{1},\ldots,\bm{f}_{p}]^{\mathsf{T}}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times d}
with
\bm{f}_{j}:=\bm{f}({\bm{w}}_{j})
.

For our formal results, we follow
Cheng & Montanari 2024
;
Misiakiewicz & Saeed 2024
;
Defilippis et al. 2024
and impose the following concentration property on the eigenfunctions of the feature map:

Assumption 1

(Concentration of the eigenfunctions)
.

There exists a constant
\mathsf{C}_{x}>0
such that for any p.s.d. operator
{\bm{A}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
with
{\rm Tr}({\bm{A}}{\bm{\Sigma}})<\infty
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(|{\bm{z}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\bm{A}}{\bm{z}}-{\rm Tr}({\bm{\Sigma}}{\bm{A}})|\geq t\|{\bm{\Sigma}}^{1/2}{\bm{A}}{\bm{\Sigma}}^{1/2}\|_{F}\big)\leq\mathsf{C}_{x}e^{-\frac{t}{\mathsf{C}_{x}}},

where
{\bm{z}}
is either
\bm{f}({\bm{w}})
or
\tilde{\bm{g}}({\bm{x}})
.

This condition subsumes several standard assumptions in the literature,
including independent sub-Gaussian coordinates, log-Sobolev concentration,
and convex Lipschitz concentration
(
Cheng & Montanari 2024
)
.
Moreover,
Defilippis et al. 2024
provide numerical evidence that
the deterministic equivalent predictions remain accurate well beyond these conditions,
including for non-linear feature maps and real-data settings, and
Misiakiewicz & Saeed 2024
develop relaxations of Assumption
1
for kernel regression. We leave similar extensions to RF models, and to the W2SG setting, to future work.

Random Feature Ridge Regression (RFRR).

Let
(y_{i},{\bm{x}}_{i})_{i\leq n}
be i.i.d. samples with
{\bm{x}}_{i}\sim\mu_{x}
and

y_{i}=f_{*}({\bm{x}}_{i})+\varepsilon_{i},\;\;f_{*}\in L^{2}({\mathcal{X}},\mu_{x}),\;\;\varepsilon_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\tau_{\varepsilon}^{2}).

(3)

The target function decomposes in the eigenbasis as (in
L^{2}
)

f_{*}({\bm{x}})=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\beta_{*,k}\phi_{k}({\bm{x}})=\langle{\bm{\beta}}_{*},{\bm{g}}\rangle,

where
\|{\bm{\beta}}_{*}\|_{2}=\|f_{*}\|_{L^{2}}<\infty
by assumption.
We fit this training data using ridge regression over the class of RF models (
1
):

\displaystyle\hat{\bm{a}}=

\displaystyle\argmin_{{\bm{a}}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}}\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big(y_{i}-\hat{f}({\bm{x}}_{i};{\bm{a}})\big)^{2}+\lambda\|{\bm{a}}\|^{2}_{2}\right\}

(4)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle({\bm{Z}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\bm{Z}}+\lambda{\bm{I}}_{p})^{-1}{\bm{Z}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\bm{y}},

where
\lambda\geq 0
is the regularization parameter and we denote the label vector
{\bm{y}}=(y_{i})_{i\in[n]}
and the feature matrix
{\bm{Z}}=(p^{-1/2}\varphi({\bm{x}}_{i};{\bm{w}}_{j}))_{i\in[n],j\in[p]}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times p}
.
The excess test error (or population risk) of RFRR is

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{{\rm test}}(\hat{f},f_{*}):=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{{\bm{x}}\sim\mu_{x}}\big[\big(f_{*}({\bm{x}})-\hat{f}({\bm{x}};\hat{\bm{a}})\big)^{2}\big]

(5)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\|{\bm{\beta}}_{*}-p^{-1/2}{\bm{F}}^{\mathsf{T}}\hat{\bm{a}}\|_{2}^{2}.

Weak-to-Strong training.

In this paper, we study a two-stage learning scheme consisting of a
teacher
model trained on the target data followed by a
student
model trained on data labeled by the teacher model.

Teacher.

We observe
n_{\mathsf{t}}
i.i.d. samples
(y_{i}^{(\mathsf{t})},{\bm{x}}_{i}^{(\mathsf{t})})_{i\leq n}
with
{\bm{x}}^{(\mathsf{t})}_{i}\sim\mu_{x}
and

y_{i}^{(\mathsf{t})}=\langle{\bm{\beta}}_{*},{\bm{g}}_{i}^{(\mathsf{t})}\rangle+\varepsilon_{i},\;\;\;\varepsilon_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\tau_{\mathsf{t}}^{2}),

where we denote
{\bm{g}}_{i}^{(\mathsf{t})}={\bm{g}}({\bm{x}}_{i}^{(\mathsf{t})})
.
We fit this data with a
teacher
model with
p_{\mathsf{t}}
i.i.d. random features
{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{t}}=[\bm{f}_{1}^{(\mathsf{t})},\ldots,\bm{f}_{p_{\mathsf{t}}}^{(\mathsf{t})}]^{\mathsf{T}}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{\mathsf{t}}\times d}
and regularization parameter
\lambda_{\mathsf{t}}\geq 0
. The teacher model is then given by

\hat{f}_{\mathsf{t}}({\bm{x}})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{p_{\mathsf{t}}}}\langle\hat{\bm{a}}_{\mathsf{t}},{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{t}}{\bm{g}}\rangle,

where
\hat{\bm{a}}_{\mathsf{t}}
is the RFRR solution (
4
):

\hat{\bm{a}}_{\mathsf{t}}=({\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{t}}+\lambda_{\mathsf{t}}{\bm{I}}_{p_{\mathsf{t}}})^{-1}{\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\bm{y}}_{\mathsf{t}},

with
{\bm{y}}_{\mathsf{t}}=(y_{i}^{(\mathsf{t})})_{i\leq n}
and
{\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{t}}={\bm{G}}_{\mathsf{t}}{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\mathsf{T}}/\sqrt{p_{\mathsf{t}}}
, where
{\bm{G}}_{\mathsf{t}}:=[{\bm{g}}_{1}^{(\mathsf{t})},\ldots,{\bm{g}}_{n_{\mathsf{t}}}^{(\mathsf{t})}]^{\mathsf{T}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{\mathsf{t}}\times d}
. The teacher excess test error is

\mathcal{R}_{{\rm test}}(\hat{f}_{\mathsf{t}},f_{*})=\|{\bm{\beta}}_{*}-p_{\mathsf{t}}^{-1/2}{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\mathsf{T}}\hat{\bm{a}}_{\mathsf{t}}\|_{2}^{2}.

(6)

Student.

We draw
n_{\mathsf{s}}
fresh inputs
({\bm{x}}^{(\mathsf{s})}_{i})_{i\leq n_{\mathsf{s}}}\sim_{\text{i.i.d.}}\mu_{x}
and label them using the teacher model:

y^{(\mathsf{s})}_{i}=\hat{f}_{\mathsf{t}}({\bm{x}}_{i}^{(\mathsf{s})})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{p_{\mathsf{t}}}}\langle{\bm{g}}_{i}^{(\mathsf{s})},{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\mathsf{T}}\hat{\bm{a}}_{\mathsf{t}}\rangle.

Define the teacher coefficient vector
{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}}:=p_{\mathsf{t}}^{-1/2}{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\mathsf{T}}\hat{\bm{a}}_{\mathsf{t}}
. We fit this data using a new
student
model with
p_{\mathsf{s}}
i.i.d. random features
{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}}=[\bm{f}_{1}^{(\mathsf{s})},\ldots,\bm{f}_{p_{\mathsf{s}}}^{(\mathsf{s})}]^{\mathsf{T}}
and regularization parameter
\lambda_{\mathsf{s}}\geq 0
:

\hat{f}_{\mathsf{s}}({\bm{x}})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{p_{\mathsf{s}}}}\langle\hat{\bm{a}}_{\mathsf{s}},{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}}{\bm{g}}\rangle,

where
\hat{\bm{a}}_{\mathsf{s}}
is the RFRR solution (
4
):

\hat{\bm{a}}_{\mathsf{s}}=({\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{s}}+\lambda_{\mathsf{s}}{\bm{I}}_{p_{\mathsf{s}}})^{-1}{\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\bm{y}}_{\mathsf{s}},

with
{\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{s}}={\bm{G}}_{\mathsf{s}}{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\mathsf{T}}/\sqrt{p_{\mathsf{s}}}
. The student excess test error is evaluated against the
original target
:

\mathcal{R}_{{\rm test}}(\hat{f}_{\mathsf{s}},f_{*})=\|{\bm{\beta}}_{*}-p_{\mathsf{s}}^{-1/2}{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\mathsf{T}}\hat{\bm{a}}_{\mathsf{s}}\|_{2}^{2}.

(7)

Our main technical contribution is to derive a
deterministic equivalent
for the student test error
and to prove a non-asymptotic approximation guarantee.
We now introduce a few quantities required to state these results.

Fixed points.

Consider integers
(n,p)\in{\mathbb{N}}^{2}
and a regularization parameter
\lambda>0
. We define
(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{2}
as the unique positive solution of the following self-consistency equations:

\displaystyle 1+\frac{n}{p}-\sqrt{\left(1-\frac{n}{p}\right)^{2}+4\frac{\lambda}{p\mu_{2}}}=

\displaystyle\frac{2}{p}{\rm Tr}({\bm{\Sigma}}({\bm{\Sigma}}+\mu_{2})^{-1}),

(8)

\displaystyle 1-\frac{n}{p}+\sqrt{\left(1-\frac{n}{p}\right)^{2}+4\frac{\lambda}{p\mu_{2}}}=

\displaystyle 2\frac{\mu_{1}}{\mu_{2}}.

We will denote by

(\mu_{\mathsf{t},1},\mu_{\mathsf{t},2})\quad\text{and}\quad(\mu_{\mathsf{s},1},\mu_{\mathsf{s},2})

(9)

the teacher and student fixed points associated to parameters
(n_{\mathsf{t}},p_{\mathsf{t}},\lambda_{\mathsf{t}})
and
(n_{\mathsf{s}},p_{\mathsf{s}},\lambda_{\mathsf{s}})
, respectively.

Functionals.

For a p.s.d. operator
{\bm{A}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
and fixed points
(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{>0}
, define the functionals

\displaystyle\Upsilon_{n,p}({\bm{A}};\mu_{1},\mu_{2})=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}{\rm Tr}({\bm{A}}{\bm{\Sigma}}({\bm{\Sigma}}+\mu_{2})^{-1})

(10)

\displaystyle-\frac{\mu_{1}}{n}

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm Tr}({\bm{A}}{\bm{\Sigma}}({\bm{\Sigma}}+\mu_{2})^{-2})}{1-\frac{1}{p}{\rm Tr}({\bm{\Sigma}}^{2}({\bm{\Sigma}}+\mu_{2})^{-2})},

\displaystyle\chi_{n,p}({\bm{A}};\mu_{2})=

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm Tr}({\bm{A}}{\bm{\Sigma}}({\bm{\Sigma}}+\mu_{2})^{-2})}{p-{\rm Tr}({\bm{\Sigma}}^{2}({\bm{\Sigma}}+\mu_{2})^{-2})}.

Note that we always have
\Upsilon_{n,p}({\bm{A}};\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\geq 0
. For
{\bm{A}}={\bm{I}}
, we simply write
\Upsilon_{n,p}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}):=\Upsilon_{n,p}({\bm{I}};\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
and
\chi_{n,p}(\mu_{2}):=\chi_{n,p}({\bm{I}};\mu_{2})
.

4
Deterministic equivalents of the test error

This section provides a sharp characterization of the excess test errors for both teacher and student models in the weak-to-strong framework introduced in Section
3
.
A deterministic equivalent for the teacher test error (
6
) was already derived in
Defilippis et al. 2024
; we briefly recall this result for completeness.

Our main technical contribution is a dimension-free
deterministic equivalent
for the student test error (
7
). This characterization is an explicit analytical expression that only depends on the problem parameters and the population eigenvalues of the feature map. In particular, we establish non-asymptotic approximation guarantees between the (random) student test error and its deterministic equivalent.

4.1
Test error of the teacher

We begin by recalling the dimension-free characterization of the teacher test error (
6
) from
Defilippis et al. 2024
.

Definition 1

(Teacher deterministic equivalent)
.

Let
(\mu_{\mathsf{t},1},\mu_{\mathsf{t},2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{>0}
be the unique positive solution of the self-consistency equations (
8
) with parameters
(n_{\mathsf{t}},p_{\mathsf{t}},\lambda_{\mathsf{t}})
.
The deterministic equivalent of the teacher test error (
6
) is

\displaystyle\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{t}}\coloneqq

\displaystyle~\langle{\bm{\beta}}_{*},{\bm{\Lambda}}_{\mathsf{t}}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}\rangle+\tau_{\mathsf{t}}^{2}\frac{\Upsilon_{\mathsf{t}}}{1-\Upsilon_{\mathsf{t}}},

(11)

\displaystyle{\bm{\Lambda}}_{\mathsf{t}}\coloneqq

\displaystyle~\frac{\mu_{\mathsf{t},2}^{2}}{1-\Upsilon_{\mathsf{t}}}\left[({\bm{\Sigma}}+\mu_{\mathsf{t},2})^{-2}+\chi_{\mathsf{t}}{\bm{\Sigma}}({\bm{\Sigma}}+\mu_{\mathsf{t},2})^{-2}\right],

where
\Upsilon_{\mathsf{t}}\coloneqq\Upsilon_{n_{\mathsf{t}},p_{\mathsf{t}}}(\mu_{\mathsf{t},1},\mu_{\mathsf{t},2})
and
\chi_{\mathsf{t}}\coloneqq\chi_{n_{\mathsf{t}},p_{\mathsf{t}}}(\mu_{\mathsf{t},2})
are defined in (
10
). The term
\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{t}}\coloneqq\langle{\bm{\beta}}_{*},{\bm{\Lambda}}_{\mathsf{t}}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}\rangle
corresponds to the
bias
of
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{t}}
and the term
\mathsf{V}_{\mathsf{t}}\coloneqq\tau_{\mathsf{t}}^{2}\frac{\Upsilon_{\mathsf{t}}}{1-\Upsilon_{\mathsf{t}}}
to its
variance
.

Defilippis et al. 2024
establish a non-asymptotic approximation guarantee between the test error (
6
) and its deterministic equivalent (
11
), under the technical condition below.

Assumption 2
.

There exists a constant
\mathsf{C}_{*}>0
such that

\frac{{\rm Tr}({\bm{\Sigma}}({\bm{\Sigma}}+\mu_{\mathsf{t},2})^{-1})}{{\rm Tr}({\bm{\Sigma}}^{2}({\bm{\Sigma}}+\mu_{\mathsf{t},2})^{-2})}\vee\frac{\langle{\bm{\beta}}_{*},({\bm{\Sigma}}+\mu_{\mathsf{t},2})^{-1}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}\rangle}{\mu_{\mathsf{t},2}\langle{\bm{\beta}}_{*},({\bm{\Sigma}}+\mu_{\mathsf{t},2})^{-2}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}\rangle}\leq\mathsf{C}_{*}.

In the following, we use the shorthand notations
(a\vee b)\coloneqq\max(a,b)
and
(a\wedge b)\coloneqq\min(a,b)
. Assumption
2
is mild and satisfied, for instance, under the source–capacity conditions introduced in Section
5
.
The approximation bounds depend on the feature spectrum
{\bm{\Sigma}}
and regularization
\lambda>0
through the following quantities:

\displaystyle r_{{\bm{\Sigma}}}(k)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{\sum_{j\geq k}\xi_{j}^{2}}{\xi^{2}_{k}},

(12)

\displaystyle M_{\bm{\Sigma}}(k)

\displaystyle\coloneqq 1+\frac{r_{{\bm{\Sigma}}}(k_{*})\vee k}{k}\log\left(r_{{\bm{\Sigma}}}(k_{*})\vee k\right),

\displaystyle\rho_{\lambda}(p)

\displaystyle\coloneqq 1+\frac{p\cdot\xi^{2}_{p_{*}}}{\lambda}M_{\bm{\Sigma}}(p),

\displaystyle\widetilde{\rho}_{\lambda}(n,p)

\displaystyle\coloneqq 1+\Big\{\frac{n\xi_{n_{*}}^{2}}{\lambda}+\frac{n}{p}\rho_{\lambda}(p)\Big\}M_{\bm{\Sigma}}(n)\mathbbm{1}_{n_{*}\leq p},

where
k_{*}\coloneqq\lfloor\eta_{*}k\rfloor
,
n_{*}\coloneqq\lfloor\eta_{*}n\rfloor
, and

p_{*}\coloneqq\lfloor\eta_{*}p\rfloor
for some
\eta_{*}\in(0,1/2)
specified in the theorem. Typically, e.g., for regularly varying spectrum, we will have
\rho_{\lambda}(p)\lesssim(\log p)^{C}/\lambda
and
\tilde{\rho}_{\lambda}(n,p)\lesssim(\log p\wedge n)^{C}/\lambda
.

For convenience, we introduce the shorthand notations:

\displaystyle\rho_{\mathsf{t}}\coloneqq

\displaystyle\rho_{p_{\mathsf{t}}\mu_{\mathsf{t},1}}(p_{\mathsf{t}}),\qquad\tilde{\rho}_{\mathsf{t}}\coloneqq\tilde{\rho}_{p_{\mathsf{t}}\lambda_{\mathsf{t}}/n_{\mathsf{t}}}(n_{\mathsf{t}},p_{\mathsf{t}}),

(13)

\displaystyle\rho_{\mathsf{s}}\coloneqq

\displaystyle\rho_{p_{\mathsf{s}}\mu_{\mathsf{s},1}}(p_{\mathsf{s}}),\qquad\tilde{\rho}_{\mathsf{s}}\coloneqq\tilde{\rho}_{p_{\mathsf{s}}\lambda_{\mathsf{s}}/n_{\mathsf{s}}}(n_{\mathsf{s}},p_{\mathsf{s}}).

We are now ready to state the deterministic equivalent approximation theorem for the teacher test error (
6
).

Theorem 1
.

(
Defilippis et al. 2024
, Theorem 3.3)

There exist absolute constants
C_{0},C_{1}>0
such that the following holds.
Under Assumptions
1
and
2
, for any
D,K>0
, there exist constants
\eta_{*}\in(0,1/2)
and
C_{*}>0
depending only on
D,K
and the constants in the assumptions, such that for all
n_{\mathsf{t}},p_{\mathsf{t}}\geq C_{*}
,
\lambda_{\mathsf{t}}>0
, and target
\|{\bm{\beta}}_{*}\|_{2}<\infty
, if

\displaystyle\lambda_{\mathsf{t}}\geq\max(n_{\mathsf{t}}^{-K},n_{\mathsf{t}}p_{\mathsf{t}}^{-1-K}),\;\;\;\tilde{\rho}_{\mathsf{t}}^{C_{0}}\leq n_{\mathsf{t}},\;\;\;\rho_{\mathsf{t}}^{C_{0}}\leq p_{\mathsf{t}},

then with probability at least
1-\min(n_{\mathsf{t}},p_{\mathsf{t}})^{-D}
,

\left|\mathcal{R}_{{\rm test}}(\hat{f}_{\mathsf{t}},f_{*})-\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{t}}\right|\leq C_{*}{\mathcal{E}}_{n_{\mathsf{t}},p_{\mathsf{t}}}\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{t}},

where the approximation rate is given by

{\mathcal{E}}_{n_{\mathsf{t}},p_{\mathsf{t}}}\coloneqq\frac{(\tilde{\rho}_{\mathsf{t}}\log n_{\mathsf{t}})^{C_{1}}}{\sqrt{n_{\mathsf{t}}}}+\frac{(\tilde{\rho}_{\mathsf{t}}\rho_{\mathsf{t}}\log p_{\mathsf{t}})^{C_{1}}}{\sqrt{p_{\mathsf{t}}}}.

This theorem is non-asymptotic, pointwise in the target function (no minimax or randomization), and dimension-free, covering in particular the infinite-dimensional regime
d=\infty
. Furthermore, the approximation is
multiplicative
, implying that the deterministic equivalent remains accurate even when the test error itself vanishes polynomially in
(n_{\mathsf{t}},p_{\mathsf{t}})
, provided
{\mathcal{E}}_{n_{\mathsf{t}},p_{\mathsf{t}}}
is sufficiently small. In particular,
{\mathcal{E}}_{n_{\mathsf{t}},p_{\mathsf{t}}}=o(1)
under the source–capacity conditions considered in Section
5
for
\gamma_{\lambda_{\mathsf{t}}}
not too large, including the optimal regularization. See
Defilippis et al. 2024
for a discussion.

4.2
Test error of the student

We now turn to the student test error (
7
) and introduce an analogous dimension-free deterministic equivalent.

Definition 2

(Student deterministic equivalent)
.

Let
(\mu_{\mathsf{t},1},\mu_{\mathsf{t},2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{>0}
and
(\mu_{\mathsf{s},1},\mu_{\mathsf{s},2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{>0}
be the unique positive solutions of the self-consistency equations (
8
) with parameters
(n_{\mathsf{t}},p_{\mathsf{t}},\lambda_{\mathsf{t}})
and
(n_{\mathsf{s}},p_{\mathsf{s}},\lambda_{\mathsf{s}})
respectively. The deterministic equivalent of the student test error is

\displaystyle\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{s}}\coloneqq

\displaystyle~\langle{\bm{\beta}}_{*},{\bm{\Lambda}}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}\rangle+\tau_{\mathsf{t}}^{2}\frac{\Upsilon_{\mathsf{t}}({\bm{\Lambda}}_{0})}{1-\Upsilon_{\mathsf{t}}},

(14)

where the p.s.d. operators
{\bm{\Lambda}}
and
{\bm{\Lambda}}_{0}
are defined by

\displaystyle{\bm{\Lambda}}\coloneqq{\bm{I}}-2{\bm{\Sigma}}^{2}({\bm{\Sigma}}+\mu_{\mathsf{s},2})^{-1}({\bm{\Sigma}}+\mu_{\mathsf{t},2})^{-1}

\displaystyle+{\bm{\Lambda}}_{0}{\bm{\Sigma}}^{2}({\bm{\Sigma}}+\mu_{\mathsf{t},2})^{-2}+\frac{\Upsilon_{\mathsf{t}}({\bm{\Lambda}}_{0})}{1-\Upsilon_{\mathsf{t}}}\mu_{\mathsf{t},2}^{2}({\bm{\Sigma}}+\mu_{\mathsf{t},2})^{-2}

\displaystyle+\left[\frac{\Upsilon_{\mathsf{t}}({\bm{\Lambda}}_{0})}{1-\Upsilon_{\mathsf{t}}}\chi_{\mathsf{t}}+\chi_{\mathsf{t}}({\bm{\Lambda}}_{0})\right]\mu_{\mathsf{t},2}^{2}{\bm{\Sigma}}({\bm{\Sigma}}+\mu_{\mathsf{t},2})^{-2},

\displaystyle{\bm{\Lambda}}_{0}\coloneqq{\bm{\Sigma}}^{2}({\bm{\Sigma}}+\mu_{\mathsf{s},2})^{-2}+\frac{\Upsilon_{\mathsf{s}}}{1-\Upsilon_{\mathsf{s}}}\mu_{\mathsf{s},2}^{2}({\bm{\Sigma}}+\mu_{\mathsf{s},2})^{-2}

\displaystyle+\frac{\chi_{\mathsf{s}}}{1-\Upsilon_{\mathsf{s}}}\mu_{\mathsf{s},2}^{2}{\bm{\Sigma}}({\bm{\Sigma}}+\mu_{\mathsf{s},2})^{-2},

and we use the shorthand notations

\displaystyle\Upsilon_{s}\coloneqq

\displaystyle\Upsilon_{n_{\mathsf{s}},p_{\mathsf{s}}}(\mu_{\mathsf{s},1},\mu_{\mathsf{s},2}),\qquad\chi_{\mathsf{s}}\coloneqq\chi_{n_{\mathsf{s}},p_{\mathsf{s}}}(\mu_{\mathsf{s},2}),

\displaystyle\Upsilon_{\mathsf{t}}({\bm{\Lambda}}_{0})\coloneqq

\displaystyle\Upsilon_{n_{\mathsf{t}},p_{\mathsf{t}}}({\bm{\Lambda}}_{0};\mu_{\mathsf{t},1},\mu_{\mathsf{t},2}),\qquad\Upsilon_{\mathsf{t}}\coloneqq\Upsilon_{\mathsf{t}}({\bm{I}}),

\displaystyle\chi_{\mathsf{t}}({\bm{\Lambda}}_{0})\coloneqq

\displaystyle\chi_{n_{\mathsf{t}},p_{\mathsf{t}}}({\bm{\Lambda}}_{0};\mu_{\mathsf{t},2}),\qquad\chi_{\mathsf{t}}\coloneqq\chi_{\mathsf{t}}({\bm{I}}).

We note that the student receives labels from the teacher without additional noise. Thus, there is no variance term in
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{s}}
, and the two terms
\mathsf{B}_{bias,\mathsf{s}}\coloneqq\langle{\bm{\beta}}_{*},{\bm{\Lambda}}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}\rangle
,
\mathsf{B}_{var,\mathsf{s}}\coloneqq\tau_{\mathsf{t}}^{2}\frac{\Upsilon_{\mathsf{t}}({\bm{\Lambda}}_{0})}{1-\Upsilon_{\mathsf{t}}}
are respectively induced by
\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{t}}
and
\mathsf{V}_{\mathsf{t}}
. We further introduce the approximation functional

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{\mathsf{s}}\coloneqq

\displaystyle\|{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}}\|_{2}\|{\bm{\beta}}_{*}-{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}}\|_{2}+\|{\bm{\beta}}_{*}\|_{2}^{2}(\|{\bm{\Lambda}}_{0}\|_{{\rm op}}\vee 1)

(15)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{n_{\mathsf{t}}}\frac{\mu_{\mathsf{t},2}}{1-\Upsilon_{\mathsf{t}}}{\rm Tr}({\bm{\Lambda}}_{0}({\bm{\Sigma}}+\mu_{\mathsf{t},2})^{-1}),

where
{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}}=p_{\mathsf{t}}^{-1/2}{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\mathsf{T}}\hat{\bm{a}}_{\mathsf{t}}
is the teacher coefficient vector.
Although
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{\mathsf{s}}
is random through
{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}}
, it is of order
O(1)
with high probability under mild conditions.

(a)

Single-index target function,
n_{\mathsf{s}}=300
.

(b)

Single-index target function,
\lambda_{\mathsf{s}}=10^{-4}
.

(c)

MNIST data,
n_{\mathsf{s}}=300
.

(d)

MNIST data,
\lambda_{\mathsf{s}}=10^{-4}
.

Figure 1
:
Excess test errors of various students
trained on teacher labels, together with the corresponding deterministic equivalents (Theorem
2
), as a function of the number of student features
p_{\mathsf{s}}
. The teacher and the student are random feature models with the same number of features (
p_{\mathsf{t}}=p_{\mathsf{s}}
), trained with the same sample size (
n_{\mathsf{t}}=n_{\mathsf{s}}
) and with the teacher regularization being half of that of the student (
\lambda_{\mathsf{t}}=\lambda_{\mathsf{s}}/2
). In the top two plots, the data is given by
{\bm{x}}_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})
and
y_{i}=\text{erf}(\langle{\bm{x}}_{i},{\bm{w}}_{*}\rangle)+\varepsilon_{i}
, with
{\bm{w}}_{*}\sim{\rm Unif}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})
, and the random feature model is
\varphi({\bm{x}};{\bm{w}})=\tanh(\langle\bx, \bw\rangle)
,
{\bm{w}}\sim{\rm Unif}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})
; in the bottom two plots, the data is obtained from the MNIST dataset and the random feature model is
\varphi({\bm{x}};{\bm{w}})=\text{erf}(\langle{\bm{x}},{\bm{w}}\rangle)
,
{\bm{w}}\sim{\rm Unif}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})
. In the left figures, we fix the sample size
n_{\mathsf{s}}
and consider three values of the regularization
\lambda_{\mathsf{s}}
(in three different colors); in the right figures, we fix the regularization
\lambda_{\mathsf{s}}
and consider three values of the sample size
n_{\mathsf{s}}
(in three different colors). We run 10 independent experiments, reporting the average and the confidence interval at
1
standard deviation. The circles represent the test errors obtained experimentally and they match well the continuous curves which represent the deterministic equivalents of Definition
2
.

We now establish an approximation guarantee between the student test error (
7
) and the deterministic equivalent (
14
).

Theorem 2
.

There exist absolute constants
C_{0},C_{1}>0
such that the following holds. Under Assumption
1
, for any
D,K>0
, there exist constants
\eta_{*}\in(0,1/2)
and
C_{*}>0
depending only on
D,K
and the constant in the assumption, such that for all
n_{\mathsf{t}},p_{\mathsf{t}},n_{\mathsf{s}},p_{\mathsf{s}}\geq C_{*}
,
\lambda_{\mathsf{t}},\lambda_{\mathsf{s}}>0
, and target
\|{\bm{\beta}}_{*}\|_{2}<\infty
, if

\displaystyle\lambda_{\mathsf{t}}\geq\max(n_{\mathsf{t}}^{-K},n_{\mathsf{t}}p_{\mathsf{t}}^{-1-K}),\;\;\;\tilde{\rho}_{\mathsf{t}}^{C_{0}}\leq n_{\mathsf{t}},\;\;\;\rho_{\mathsf{t}}^{C_{0}}\leq p_{\mathsf{t}},

\displaystyle\lambda_{\mathsf{s}}\geq\max(n_{\mathsf{s}}^{-K},n_{\mathsf{s}}p_{\mathsf{s}}^{-1-K}),\;\;\;\tilde{\rho}_{\mathsf{s}}^{C_{0}}\leq n_{\mathsf{s}},\;\;\;\rho_{\mathsf{s}}^{C_{0}}\leq p_{\mathsf{s}},

then with probability at least
1-\min(n_{\mathsf{t}},p_{\mathsf{t}},n_{\mathsf{s}},p_{\mathsf{s}})^{-D}
,

\begin{split}|\mathcal{R}_{{\rm test}}(\hat{f}_{\mathsf{s}},f_{*})-\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{s}}|\leq C_{*}{\mathcal{E}}_{n_{\mathsf{s}},p_{\mathsf{s}},n_{\mathsf{t}},p_{\mathsf{t}}}\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{\mathsf{s}},\end{split}

where the approximation rate is given by

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{n_{\mathsf{s}},p_{\mathsf{s}},n_{\mathsf{t}},p_{\mathsf{t}}}\coloneqq

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathcal{E}}_{n_{\mathsf{t}},p_{\mathsf{t}}}+\frac{(\tilde{\rho}_{\mathsf{s}}\log n_{\mathsf{s}})^{C_{1}}}{\sqrt{n_{\mathsf{s}}}}+\frac{(\tilde{\rho}_{\mathsf{s}}\rho_{\mathsf{s}}\log p_{\mathsf{s}})^{C_{1}}}{\sqrt{p_{\mathsf{s}}}}.

The proof (deferred to Appendix
A
) proceeds in two stages:
(i)
a deterministic equivalent for the student conditional on the teacher coefficients
{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}}
, and
(ii)
a deterministic equivalent for
{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}}
itself. These steps rely on approximation results for functionals of random features from
(
Misiakiewicz & Saeed 2024
;
Defilippis et al. 2024
)
, together with new bounds for asymmetric functionals (stated in Appendix
B
and proved in Appendix
C
).

Discussion.

The key difference with Theorem
1
is that the error bound of Theorem
2
is
additive
rather than multiplicative, with potentially

\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{\mathsf{s}}\gg\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{s}}
.
This is due to the more intricate test error induced by the two-stage learning pipeline, with asymmetric terms. We conjecture that a multiplicative bound of the form

\big|\mathcal{R}_{{\rm test}}(\hat{f}_{\mathsf{s}},f_{*})-\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{s}}\big|\leq C_{*}{\mathcal{E}}_{n_{\mathsf{s}},p_{\mathsf{s}},n_{\mathsf{t}},p_{\mathsf{t}}}\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{s}}

holds for a broad class of feature covariances
{\bm{\Sigma}}
and targets
{\bm{\beta}}_{*}
. We leave this as a technically challenging problem for future work.

Nevertheless, Theorem
2
already yields a sharp characterization of the student risk whenever
{\mathcal{E}}_{n_{\mathsf{s}},p_{\mathsf{s}},n_{\mathsf{t}},p_{\mathsf{t}}}\,\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{\mathsf{s}}\ll\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{s}}
. Under mild conditions,
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{\mathsf{s}}=O(1)
with high probability, and

{\mathcal{E}}_{n_{\mathsf{s}},p_{\mathsf{s}},n_{\mathsf{t}},p_{\mathsf{t}}}=\tilde{O}\big(\min(n_{\mathsf{t}},n_{\mathsf{s}},p_{\mathsf{t}},p_{\mathsf{s}})^{-1/2}\big)
for a broad range of feature covariances
{\bm{\Sigma}}
and regularization parameters
\lambda_{\mathsf{s}},\lambda_{\mathsf{t}}
. In particular, by taking
1
1

1

In this regime, the minimax rate is close to
0
and one can choose the parameters in (
17
) such that
(p_{\mathsf{t}}\mu_{\mathsf{t},1},p_{\mathsf{s}}\mu_{\mathsf{s},1},p_{\mathsf{t}}\lambda_{\mathsf{t}}/n_{\mathsf{t}},p_{\mathsf{s}}\lambda_{\mathsf{s}}/n_{\mathsf{s}})
appearing in the denominator of
{\mathcal{E}}_{n_{\mathsf{s}},p_{\mathsf{s}},n_{\mathsf{t}},p_{\mathsf{t}}}
decay at rates close to
0
, slower than
\min(n_{\mathsf{t}},n_{\mathsf{s}},p_{\mathsf{t}},p_{\mathsf{s}})^{-1/2}
.

r
close to
0
and
\alpha
close to
1
, the result covers various W2SG regimes under the source–capacity conditions of Section
5
, including settings where the student improves upon the teacher’s scaling law and settings where the teacher risk does not even decay with the sample size while the student is minimax optimal.
More broadly, we expect the deterministic equivalents to remain accurate well beyond the regimes covered by Theorem
2
and beyond the assumptions made therein. To showcase this, Figure
1
shows that the deterministic equivalent in Definition
2
closely tracks the behavior of the student test error for a non-linear feature map and data coming from either a single-index target function (top plots) or from a standard dataset (MNIST, bottom plots). In both cases, the deterministic equivalents are evaluated by estimating
({\bm{\beta}}_{*},{\bm{\Sigma}})
from a large dataset (see Appendix C.3 in
Defilippis et al. 2024
).

5
Scaling laws for the test error

Following prior work on scaling laws in linear and random-feature models

(
Maloney et al. 2022
;
Bahri et al. 2024
;
Defilippis et al. 2024
;
Paquette et al. 2024
;
Lin et al. 2024
)
,
we adopt the classical
source
and
capacity
conditions

(
Caponnetto & De Vito 2007
;
Rudi & Rosasco 2017
)
.
Specifically, we assume a power-law spectrum and target coefficients:

\begin{split}{\bm{\Sigma}}_{k,k}=k^{-\alpha},\qquad{\bm{\beta}}_{*,k}=k^{-\frac{(1+2\alpha r)}{2}},\end{split}

(16)

where
\alpha>1
and
r>0
.
For instance,
r>\tfrac{1}{2}
corresponds to
f_{*}
belonging to the RKHS of the RF kernel.

Scaling of model widths and regularizations.

We consider the two-stage teacher–student procedure with
n_{\mathsf{t}}
ground-truth samples in the teacher stage.
We parameterize the remaining quantities as power laws in
n_{\mathsf{t}}
:

\begin{split}&p_{\mathsf{t}}=n_{\mathsf{t}}^{\gamma_{p_{\mathsf{t}}}},\quad\lambda_{\mathsf{t}}=n_{\mathsf{t}}^{-\gamma_{\lambda_{\mathsf{t}}}};\\
&p_{\mathsf{s}}=n_{\mathsf{t}}^{\gamma_{p_{\mathsf{s}}}},\quad n_{\mathsf{s}}=n_{\mathsf{t}}^{\gamma_{n_{\mathsf{s}}}},\quad\lambda_{\mathsf{s}}=n_{\mathsf{t}}^{-\gamma_{\lambda_{\mathsf{s}}}}.\end{split}

(17)

Using the deterministic equivalents (
11
) and (
14
),
we derive sharp decay rates for the teacher and student test errors as
n_{\mathsf{t}}\to\infty
under source–capacity conditions (
16
) and hyperparameter scaling (
17
).

To state these rates, it will be convenient to introduce

z_{\mathsf{s}}=\Big(\frac{\gamma_{n_{\mathsf{s}}}+\gamma_{\lambda_{\mathsf{s}}}}{\alpha}\wedge\gamma_{n_{\mathsf{s}}}\wedge\gamma_{p_{\mathsf{s}}}\Big),\;z_{\mathsf{t}}=\Big(\frac{1+\gamma_{\lambda_{\mathsf{t}}}}{\alpha}\wedge 1\wedge\gamma_{p_{\mathsf{t}}}\Big).

Teacher scaling laws.

The scaling laws for the teacher model were derived in
Defilippis et al. 2024
; we restate them here for completeness.

Theorem 3
.

(
Defilippis et al. 2024
, Theorem 4.1)

Under the scaling assumptions (
16
) and (
17
), the teacher deterministic equivalent
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{t}}=\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{t}}+\mathsf{V}_{\mathsf{t}}
satisfies

\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{t}}=\Theta(n_{\mathsf{t}}^{-\gamma_{\mathsf{t},\mathsf{B}}}),\qquad\mathsf{V}_{\mathsf{t}}=\Theta(\tau_{\mathsf{t}}^{2}n_{\mathsf{t}}^{-\gamma_{\mathsf{t},\mathsf{V}}}),

where

\displaystyle\gamma_{\mathsf{t},\mathsf{B}}\coloneqq

\displaystyle[2\alpha(r\wedge 1)z_{\mathsf{t}}]\wedge[\gamma_{p_{\mathsf{t}}}+(2\alpha(r\wedge 1/2)-1)z_{\mathsf{t}}],

\displaystyle\gamma_{\mathsf{t},\mathsf{V}}\coloneqq

\displaystyle 1-z_{\mathsf{t}}.

Furthermore, if
\tau_{\mathsf{t}}^{2}=\Theta(1),
the optimal teacher exponent is

\begin{split}\gamma_{\mathsf{t},*}=\max_{p_{\mathsf{t}},\lambda_{\mathsf{t}}}\{\gamma_{\mathsf{t},\mathsf{B}}\wedge\gamma_{\mathsf{t},\mathsf{V}}\}=\frac{2\alpha(r\wedge 1)}{1+2\alpha(r\wedge 1)},\end{split}

and it is achieved when
z_{\mathsf{t}}=\frac{1}{1+2\alpha(r\wedge 1)}
and
\gamma_{p_{\mathsf{t}}}\geq 1-\frac{2\alpha(r\wedge\frac{1}{2})}{1+2\alpha(r\wedge 1)}
.

Student scaling laws.

We now derive the scaling laws for the student model using the deterministic equivalent of
Theorem
2
.

Theorem 4

(Student scaling laws)
.

Under the scaling assumptions (
16
) and (
17
), the student deterministic equivalent
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{s}}=\mathsf{B}_{bias,\mathsf{s}}+\mathsf{B}_{var,\mathsf{s}}
satisfies

\mathsf{B}_{bias,\mathsf{s}}=\Theta(n_{\mathsf{t}}^{-\gamma_{\mathsf{s},bias}}),\quad\mathsf{B}_{var,\mathsf{s}}=\Theta(\tau_{\mathsf{t}}^{2}n_{\mathsf{t}}^{-\gamma_{\mathsf{s},var}}),

where

\displaystyle\gamma_{\mathsf{s},bias}\hskip-3.00003pt\coloneqq\hskip-3.00003pt\begin{cases}&\hskip-8.00003pt[2\alpha(r\wedge 1)z_{\mathsf{t}}]\wedge[\gamma_{p_{\mathsf{t}}}+(2\alpha(r\wedge\frac{1}{2})-1)z_{\mathsf{t}}]\\
&\hskip-8.00003pt\wedge[(2\alpha(r\wedge\frac{1}{2})\hskip-1.99997pt-\hskip-1.99997pt\alpha)z_{\mathsf{t}}\hskip-1.99997pt+\hskip-1.99997pt(\alpha\hskip-1.99997pt-\hskip-1.99997pt1)z_{\mathsf{s}}\hskip-1.99997pt+\hskip-1.99997pt\gamma_{p_{\mathsf{s}}}],\,\text{for $z_{\mathsf{t}}\leq z_{s}$}\\
&\hskip-8.00003pt[2\alpha(r\wedge 1)z_{\mathsf{s}}]\wedge[2\alpha(r\wedge\frac{1}{2})z_{\mathsf{s}}-z_{\mathsf{s}}+\gamma_{p_{\mathsf{s}}}]\\
&\hskip-8.00003pt\wedge[2\alpha(r\wedge\frac{1}{2})z_{\mathsf{t}}+\alpha(z_{\mathsf{t}}-z_{\mathsf{s}})-z_{\mathsf{s}}+\gamma_{p_{\mathsf{t}}}]\\
&\hskip-8.00003pt\wedge[2\alpha(r\wedge\frac{1}{2})z_{\mathsf{t}}-z_{\mathsf{t}}+1-z_{\mathsf{s}}+\gamma_{p_{\mathsf{t}}}],\,\text{for $z_{\mathsf{t}}>z_{\mathsf{s}}$}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\gamma_{\mathsf{s},var}\coloneqq 1-(z_{\mathsf{s}}\wedge z_{\mathsf{t}}).

Furthermore, if
\tau_{\mathsf{t}}^{2}=\Theta(1),
the optimal student exponent is

\begin{split}\gamma_{\mathsf{s},*}:=&\max_{p_{\mathsf{t}},\lambda_{\mathsf{t}},n_{\mathsf{s}},p_{\mathsf{s}},\lambda_{\mathsf{s}}}\{\gamma_{\mathsf{s},bias}\wedge\gamma_{\mathsf{s},var}\}=\frac{2\alpha\left(r\wedge 1\right)}{1+2\alpha\left(r\wedge 1\right)},\end{split}

and it is achieved when
(z_{\mathsf{s}}\wedge z_{\mathsf{t}})=\frac{1}{1+2\alpha(r\wedge 1)}
and one of the following two conditions is satisfied:

1.

z_{\mathsf{t}}\leq z_{\mathsf{s}}
and

\begin{split}\gamma_{p_{\mathsf{s}}}&\geq(1-\alpha)z_{\mathsf{s}}+\frac{2\alpha(r\wedge 1)-2\alpha(r\wedge\frac{1}{2})+\alpha}{1+2\alpha(r\wedge 1)},\\
\gamma_{p_{\mathsf{t}}}&\geq 1-\frac{2\alpha(r\wedge\frac{1}{2})}{1+2\alpha(r\wedge 1)}.\end{split}

(18)

2.

z_{\mathsf{t}}>z_{\mathsf{s}}
and

\begin{split}&\gamma_{p_{\mathsf{s}}}\geq 1-\frac{2\alpha(r\wedge\frac{1}{2})}{1+2\alpha(r\wedge 1)},\\
&z_{\mathsf{s}}\leq\frac{1}{\alpha}(2\alpha(r\wedge\frac{1}{2})z_{\mathsf{t}}+\gamma_{p_{\mathsf{t}}}+\alpha z_{\mathsf{t}}-1),\\
&2\alpha(r\wedge\frac{1}{2})z_{\mathsf{t}}+\gamma_{p_{\mathsf{t}}}-z_{\mathsf{t}}\geq 0.\end{split}

(19)

The proof of Theorem
4
is deferred to Appendix
G.2
.

Stable regularization.

For any fixed
(\gamma_{p_{\mathsf{t}}},\gamma_{p_{\mathsf{s}}},\gamma_{n_{\mathsf{s}}})
, we call

(\gamma_{\lambda_{\mathsf{t}}},\gamma_{\lambda_{\mathsf{s}}})

stable
if
\gamma_{\lambda_{\mathsf{t}}}\geq-1
and
\gamma_{\lambda_{\mathsf{s}}}\geq-\gamma_{n_{\mathsf{s}}}
.
This condition implies that
z_{\mathsf{t}},z_{\mathsf{s}}\geq 0
, ensuring that student and teacher risks
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{t}}
and
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{s}}
remain
O(1)
(i.e., do not diverge).
Without loss of generality, we restrict our attention to stable regularizations in the following.

Optimal and minimax rates.

For any fixed
(\alpha,r)
, the best achievable exponent for the student and the teacher are equal
\gamma_{\mathsf{s},*}=\gamma_{\mathsf{t},*}
. In particular, a student trained
only
on teacher-generated labels cannot outperform the
optimally tuned
teacher trained on ground-truth labels in terms of asymptotic scaling. It is also useful to compare to the minimax rate under source–capacity conditions, which is
n_{\mathsf{t}}^{-\frac{2\alpha r}{1+2\alpha r}}

(
Caponnetto & De Vito 2007
)
. Thus, an optimally tuned student trained
only
on teacher-generated labels achieves the minimax rate when
r\leq 1
, same as the optimally tuned teacher trained on ground-truth labels.

5.1
Weak-to-Strong Generalization

We now leverage the teacher and student scaling laws in Theorems
3
and
4
to address the central question of this paper:

When can a student achieve a better scaling law than its teacher in the RFRR setting?

Necessary condition for weak-to-strong generalization.

Our first observation is a necessary condition for the improvement in scaling law under the source–capacity model, which immediately follows by inspecting the decay rates in Theorems
3
and
4
.

Corollary 1
.

Take any
\alpha>1
,
r>0
, and any relative scaling coefficients
(\gamma_{p_{\mathsf{t}}},\gamma_{p_{\mathsf{s}}},\gamma_{n_{\mathsf{s}}},\gamma_{\lambda_{\mathsf{t}}},\gamma_{\lambda_{\mathsf{s}}})
. On the one hand, if
z_{\mathsf{t}}\leq z_{\mathsf{s}},
then

\mathsf{B}_{bias,\mathsf{s}}=\Omega(\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{t}}),\qquad\mathsf{B}_{var,\mathsf{s}}=\Theta(\mathsf{V}_{\mathsf{t}}).

On the other hand, if
z_{\mathsf{t}}>z_{\mathsf{s}}
, then
\mathsf{B}_{var,\mathsf{s}}=o(\mathsf{V}_{\mathsf{t}})
.

Corollary
1
implies that the improvement in scaling law is possible only if
z_{\mathsf{t}}>z_{\mathsf{s}}
. In fact, if
z_{\mathsf{t}}\leq z_{\mathsf{s}}
, then the bias/variance of the teacher induces error terms at least of the same order in the student (with
\mathsf{B}_{bias,\mathsf{s}}
being potentially larger than
\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{t}}
).
Below, we discuss two mechanisms leading to weak-to-strong generalization:
variance reduction
and
bias reduction
.

Variance reduction.

When the teacher is variance-dominated, with
\mathsf{V}_{\mathsf{t}}\gg\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{t}}
, a student can
always
improve the scaling law of the teacher by reducing the variance term.

Corollary 2
.

Assume
\tau_{\mathsf{t}}^{2}=\Theta(1)
. Take
\alpha>1
,
r>0
, and teacher scalings
(\gamma_{p_{\mathsf{t}}},\gamma_{\lambda_{\mathsf{t}}})
such that
\mathsf{V}_{\mathsf{t}}\gg\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{t}}
, i.e.,

\begin{split}z_{\mathsf{t}}>\frac{1}{1+2\alpha(r\wedge 1)}\vee\frac{1-\gamma_{p_{\mathsf{t}}}}{2\alpha(r\wedge\frac{1}{2})},\;\;\gamma_{p_{\mathsf{t}}}>\frac{1}{1+2\alpha(r\wedge\frac{1}{2})}.\end{split}

(20)

Then, the student achieves a better scaling law than its teacher (namely,
\gamma_{\mathsf{s},bias}\wedge\gamma_{\mathsf{s},var}>\gamma_{\mathsf{t},\mathsf{B}}\wedge\gamma_{\mathsf{t},\mathsf{V}}
) if and only if

\begin{split}2\alpha(r\wedge 1/2)z_{\mathsf{s}}-z_{\mathsf{s}}+\gamma_{p_{\mathsf{s}}}>1-z_{\mathsf{t}},\quad z_{\mathsf{t}}>z_{\mathsf{s}}>\frac{1-z_{\mathsf{t}}}{2\alpha(r\wedge 1)}.\end{split}

Corollary
2
(proved in Appendix
G.3
) characterizes the W2SG region when the teacher is variance-dominated, in terms of
(z_{\mathsf{t}},z_{\mathsf{s}},\gamma_{p_{\mathsf{t}}},\gamma_{p_{\mathsf{s}}})
.
In particular, by taking a student such that

z_{\mathsf{s}}=\frac{1}{1+2\alpha(r\wedge 1)},\qquad\gamma_{p_{\mathsf{s}}}\geq 1-\frac{2\alpha(r\wedge\frac{1}{2})}{1+2\alpha(r\wedge 1)},

we have that, for a variance-dominated teacher, (
19
) holds. Hence, by Theorem
4
, the student achieves the optimal rate
\gamma_{\mathsf{s},*}
. A notable consequence is that when
0<r<1
,
the student can achieve the minimax exponent under source–capacity conditions even if the teacher does not
(e.g., when due to mis-regularization,
z_{\mathsf{t}}
is too large and the teacher is variance-limited).
In fact, the student can achieve the minimax rate even in regimes where the teacher variance exponent satisfies
\gamma_{\mathsf{t},\mathsf{V}}=0
(i.e. the teacher risk does not decay with
n_{\mathsf{t}}
).
This illustrates that W2SG improvements can be substantial in RFRR.

Bias reduction.

Even if the teacher is not variance-dominated, i.e.,
\mathsf{V}_{\mathsf{t}}\leq\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{t}}
, it is still possible to improve the scaling law, and the corollary below (proved in Appendix
G.4
) characterizes the W2SG region in this setting.

Corollary 3
.

Take
\alpha>1
,
r>0
, teacher scalings
(\gamma_{p_{\mathsf{t}}},\gamma_{\lambda_{\mathsf{t}}})
and
\tau_{t}
such that
\mathsf{V}_{\mathsf{t}}\leq\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{t}}
. Then, the student achieves a better scaling law than its teacher (namely,
\gamma_{\mathsf{s},bias}\wedge\gamma_{\mathsf{s},var}>\gamma_{\mathsf{t},\mathsf{B}}\wedge\gamma_{\mathsf{t},\mathsf{V}}
) if and only if

\begin{split}&z_{\mathsf{t}}\in\Big(\frac{\gamma_{p_{\mathsf{t}}}}{1+2\alpha(r\wedge 1)-2\alpha(r\wedge\frac{1}{2})},\\
&\qquad\qquad\gamma_{p_{\mathsf{t}}}\wedge\frac{1}{\alpha-1}\left(\frac{2\alpha(r\wedge 1)}{1+2\alpha(r\wedge 1)}-\gamma_{p_{\mathsf{t}}}\right)\Big),\\
&\gamma_{p_{\mathsf{t}}}<1-\frac{2\alpha(r\wedge\frac{1}{2})}{1+2\alpha(r\wedge 1)},\\
&1-(\alpha-1)z_{\mathsf{t}}-\gamma_{p_{\mathsf{t}}}>z_{\mathsf{s}}>\frac{(\alpha-1)z_{\mathsf{t}}+\gamma_{p_{\mathsf{t}}}}{2\alpha(r\wedge 1)},\\
&\gamma_{p_{\mathsf{s}}}-\gamma_{p_{\mathsf{t}}}>(\alpha-1)(z_{\mathsf{t}}-z_{\mathsf{s}}).\end{split}

We note that W2SG can only happen if
(i)

r>1/2
and
(ii)
the student width is larger than the teacher width
\gamma_{p_{\mathsf{s}}}>\gamma_{p_{\mathsf{t}}}
. Notably, in this regime, the scaling law can be improved even when
\tau_{\mathsf{t}}^{2}=0,
i.e., the teacher is trained with clean labels.

Discussion.

Corollaries
2
-
3
above identify a set of conditions on the number of training samples, model size and regularization allowing the student to improve upon the scaling law of the teacher. To interpret such conditions, we focus on two special cases:
(i)
the
kernel limit
, in which both teacher and student have a very large number of features, i.e.,
\gamma_{p_{\mathsf{t}}}=\gamma_{p_{\mathsf{s}}}=\infty
, and
(ii)
the
approximation limit
, in which the student has a very large number of samples labeled by the teacher, i.e.,
\gamma_{n_{\mathsf{s}}}=\infty
.

In the
kernel limit
, W2SG improvements come only from variance reduction, since the upper bound on
\gamma_{p_{\mathsf{t}}}
required by Corollary
3
does not hold. Furthermore, the student improves the scaling law only of teachers that are not already optimal, due to a sub-optimal choice of the regularization. Formally, this corresponds to
z_{\mathsf{t}}
larger than its optimal value
\frac{1}{1+2\alpha(r\wedge 1)}
(Theorem
3
shows that the optimal teacher exponent is reached when
z_{\mathsf{t}}=\frac{1}{1+2\alpha(r\wedge 1)}
, and (
20
) requires
z_{\mathsf{t}}>\frac{1}{1+2\alpha(r\wedge 1)}
). Now, for any sub-optimal teacher, W2SG holds upon choosing properly sample size and regularization of the student:

(a)

If the student has enough samples (
\gamma_{n_{\mathsf{s}}}>z_{\mathsf{t}}
, implied when the student has more samples than the teacher), the scaling law improvement occurs for a range of regularizations (
\gamma_{\lambda_{\mathsf{s}}}\in(\frac{1-z_{\mathsf{t}}}{2(r\wedge 1)}-\gamma_{n_{\mathsf{s}}},\alpha z_{\mathsf{t}}-\gamma_{n_{\mathsf{s}}})
).

(b)

For small regularizations (
\gamma_{\lambda_{\mathsf{s}}}\geq(\alpha-1)\gamma_{n_{\mathsf{s}}}
), the scaling law improvement occurs for a range of sample sizes (
\gamma_{n_{\mathsf{s}}}\in(\frac{1-z_{\mathsf{t}}}{2\alpha(r\wedge 1)},z_{\mathsf{t}})
); the improvement also occurs for large regularizations (
\gamma_{\lambda_{\mathsf{s}}}<(\alpha-1)\gamma_{n_{\mathsf{s}}}
), albeit in a different range of sample sizes (
\gamma_{n_{\mathsf{s}}}\in(\frac{1-z_{\mathsf{t}}}{2(r\wedge 1)}-\gamma_{\lambda_{\mathsf{s}}},\alpha z_{\mathsf{t}}-\gamma_{\lambda_{\mathsf{s}}})
).

In the
approximation limit
, W2SG improvements can come from either variance or bias reduction. Given that the number of student samples is large, its regularization becomes irrelevant (
\gamma_{n_{\mathsf{s}}}=\infty
implies
z_{\mathsf{s}}=\gamma_{p_{\mathsf{s}}}
and, hence,
\gamma_{\lambda_{\mathsf{s}}}
does not appear in any of the conditions of Corollaries
2
-
3
). Thus, the only relevant student quantity is its number of features, which has to be chosen carefully in order to improve the scaling law. As mentioned earlier, when W2SG occurs due to a bias reduction, the student width has to be larger than the teacher width; in contrast, when W2SG occurs due to a variance reduction, the student width has to be smaller than the teacher width.

(a)

Variance dominated: W2SG.

(b)

Bias dominated: W2SG.

(c)

Optimally tuned teacher: no W2SG.

Figure 2
:
Excess test errors of various students and teachers, as a function of the number of ground-truth samples
n_{\mathsf{t}}
. We consider random feature ridge regression with weak-to-strong training, under the source and capacity conditions in (
16
) and with the hyperparameter scaling in (
17
). Each plot corresponds to a different choice of
(\alpha,r,\gamma_{p_{\mathsf{t}}},\gamma_{\lambda_{\mathsf{t}}})
. Points are empirical experiments, solid lines are deterministic equivalent predictions (Theorems
1
and
2
), dotted lines are theoretical decay rates (Theorems
3
and
4
), and the grey dashed line is the minimax rate. The teacher test error is in blue, while the other colors correspond to student test errors for different choices of
(\gamma_{n_{\mathsf{s}}},\gamma_{p_{\mathsf{s}}},\gamma_{\lambda_{\mathsf{s}}})
.

Numerical results.

We illustrate our theory via the simulations of Figure
2
. We pick a Gaussian linear model, i.e.,
{\bm{g}}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,{\bm{I}}),\bm{f}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,{\bm{\Sigma}}),
with
d=20000.
For different choices of
(\alpha,r,\gamma_{p_{\mathsf{t}}},\gamma_{\lambda_{\mathsf{t}}})
, we compare the test error of the teacher (in blue), the test error of various students (in other colors), and the minimax rate (the grey dashed line with slope
-\frac{2\alpha r}{1+2\alpha r}
). For each teacher and student, we plot points for the empirical test error (Equations (
6
) and (
7
)), solid lines for deterministic equivalent predictions (Theorems
1
and
2
), and dotted lines for theoretical decay rates (Theorems
3
and
4
). We run
25
independent experiments, reporting the average and the confidence interval at
1
standard deviation. We note that, in all the cases considered, the theoretical predictions are in excellent agreement with the empirical test errors. Each of the three subplots corresponds to a different scenario:

(a)

Variance-dominated regime:
The teacher test error does not decay with
n_{\mathsf{t}}
(zero decay rate), while a properly tuned student (in red) achieves the minimax rate.

(b)

Bias-dominated regime (
\tau_{\mathsf{t}}^{2}=0
):
Two of the three students reduce the bias, thus improving the scaling law.

(c)

Optimally regularized teacher:
The teacher achieves the minimax rate, and no student can improve the scaling law.

6
Conclusion

In this paper, we show that, in random feature ridge regression, a student trained on labels generated via a teacher can attain a scaling law which is more favorable than that of the teacher itself. This demonstrates the benefit of proper regularization and over-parameterization in the context of weak-to-strong generalization, since the same claim does not hold for ridgeless linear regression
(
Ildiz et al. 2025
)
. We obtain this result through the derivation of a novel deterministic equivalent for the excess test error of the student after a two-stage learning procedure. In contrast with deterministic equivalents obtained in earlier work
(
Defilippis et al. 2024
;
Misiakiewicz & Saeed 2024
)
, our derivation is made challenging by the necessity to handle two sources of randomness (from both the student and the teacher), and we expect the analysis carried out in this paper to be applicable to various problems in high-dimensional statistics involving multiple data sources, including distribution shift
(
Patil et al. 2024
;
Mallinar et al. 2024
)
and transfer learning
(
Yang et al. 2025
;
Song et al. 2024
)
.

Impact Statement

This paper presents work whose goal is to advance the field of Machine
Learning. There are many potential societal consequences of our work, none of
which we feel must be specifically highlighted here.

Notation

Recall that we denote
{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{t}}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{\mathsf{t}}\times d}
the teacher random features with i.i.d. rows with covariance
{\bm{\Sigma}}
,
{\bm{G}}_{\mathsf{t}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{\mathsf{t}}\times d}
the teacher input data with i.i.d. rows with covariance
{\bm{I}}_{d}
,
{\bm{\beta}}_{*}
the target function coefficients, such that
y_{i}=\langle{\bm{\beta}}_{*},{\bm{g}}_{\mathsf{t},i}\rangle+\varepsilon_{i}
, and
\hat{\bm{a}}_{\mathsf{t}}
the solution of RFRR. We denote
{\bm{y}}_{\mathsf{t}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{\mathsf{t}}}
the vector of labels from the true target, and
{\bm{\varepsilon}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{\mathsf{t}}}
the vector of noise, such that

{\bm{y}}_{\mathsf{t}}={\bm{G}}_{\mathsf{t}}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}+{\bm{\varepsilon}}.

We further define

{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{p_{\mathsf{t}}}}{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\mathsf{T}}\hat{\bm{a}}_{\mathsf{t}},\qquad{\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{t}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{p_{\mathsf{t}}}}{\bm{G}}_{\mathsf{t}}{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\mathsf{T}}.

For the student model, we define the corresponding
{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}},{\bm{G}}_{\mathsf{s}},{\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{s}},\hat{\bm{a}}_{\mathsf{s}}
in the same way.

Recall that the student has solution

\hat{\bm{a}}_{\mathsf{s}}=({\bm{Z}}^{\mathsf{T}}_{\mathsf{s}}{\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{s}}+\lambda_{\mathsf{s}})^{-1}{\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\bm{y}}_{\mathsf{s}},

where
{\bm{y}}_{\mathsf{s}}
is the data coming from the teacher given by

{\bm{y}}_{\mathsf{s}}={\bm{G}}_{\mathsf{s}}{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}}.

We denote

\bm{\Xi}_{\mathsf{s}}:=({\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{s}}+\lambda_{\mathsf{s}})^{-1},\qquad\widehat{{\bm{\Sigma}}}_{\mathsf{s}}:=\frac{{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}}{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\mathsf{T}}}{p_{\mathsf{s}}}.

The test error of the student model can be characterized as

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}(f_{*};{\bm{G}}_{\mathsf{s}},{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}},\lambda,{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}})=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\bm{g}}\left[\left({\bm{g}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\bm{\beta}}_{*}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{p_{\mathsf{s}}}}{\bm{g}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\mathsf{T}}\hat{\bm{a}}_{\mathsf{s}}\right)^{2}\right]=:\mathcal{B}_{\mathsf{s}}({\bm{\beta}}_{*},{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}};{\bm{G}}_{\mathsf{s}},{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}},\lambda_{\mathsf{s}}),

where we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{\mathsf{s}}({\bm{\beta}}_{*},{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}};{\bm{G}}_{\mathsf{s}},{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}},\lambda_{\mathsf{s}}):=

\displaystyle\|{\bm{\beta}}_{*}-p_{\mathsf{s}}^{-1/2}{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Xi}_{\mathsf{s}}{\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\bm{G}}_{\mathsf{s}}{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}}\|_{2}^{2},

We also define the following error scaling term:

\begin{split}&{\mathcal{E}}_{n_{\mathsf{s}},p_{\mathsf{s}}}:=\frac{(\tilde{\rho}_{\mathsf{s}}\log n_{\mathsf{s}})^{C_{1}}}{\sqrt{n_{\mathsf{s}}}}+\frac{(\tilde{\rho}_{\mathsf{s}}\rho_{\mathsf{s}}\log p_{\mathsf{s}})^{C_{1}}}{\sqrt{p_{\mathsf{s}}}},\qquad{\mathcal{E}}_{n_{\mathsf{t}},p_{\mathsf{t}}}:=\frac{(\tilde{\rho}_{\mathsf{t}}\log n_{\mathsf{t}})^{C_{1}}}{\sqrt{n_{\mathsf{t}}}}+\frac{(\tilde{\rho}_{\mathsf{t}}\rho_{\mathsf{t}}\log p_{\mathsf{t}})^{C_{1}}}{\sqrt{p_{\mathsf{t}}}};\\
&{\mathcal{E}}_{n_{\mathsf{s}},p_{\mathsf{s}}}:={\mathcal{E}}_{n_{\mathsf{s}},p_{\mathsf{s}}}+{\mathcal{E}}_{n_{\mathsf{t}},p_{\mathsf{t}}}\end{split}

Appendix A
Proof of Theorem
2
: Deterministic equivalent for the student

In order to prove the deterministic equivalent of the student’s test error, we first compute the deterministic equivalent of the test error conditioned on the teacher’s weights
{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}},
and then compute the deterministic equivalent w.r.t
{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}}.

In particular, we prove that, with probability at least
1-n^{-D}
,

\begin{split}&|\mathcal{B}_{\mathsf{s}}({\bm{\beta}}_{*},{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}};{\bm{G}}_{\mathsf{s}},{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}},\lambda_{\mathsf{s}})-\mathcal{B}_{\mathsf{s}}({\bm{\beta}}_{*},{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}})|\leq C_{*}{\mathcal{E}}_{n_{\mathsf{s}},p_{\mathsf{s}}}\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathsf{s}}({\bm{\beta}}_{*},{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}})\\
&|\mathcal{B}_{\mathsf{s}}({\bm{\beta}}_{*},{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}})-\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{s}}({\bm{\beta}}_{*})|\leq C_{*}{\mathcal{E}}_{n_{\mathsf{t}},p_{\mathsf{t}}}\tilde{\mathsf{B}}_{\mathsf{s}}({\bm{\beta}}_{*})\end{split}

and then by triangle inequality, we have

\begin{split}|\mathcal{B}_{\mathsf{s}}({\bm{\beta}}_{*},{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}};{\bm{G}}_{\mathsf{s}},{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}},\lambda_{\mathsf{s}})-\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{s}}({\bm{\beta}}_{*})|\leq C_{*}{\mathcal{E}}_{n_{\mathsf{s}},p_{\mathsf{s}},n_{\mathsf{t}},p_{\mathsf{t}}}(\left\{\mathcal{B}_{\mathsf{s}}({\bm{\beta}}_{*},{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}})+\tilde{\mathsf{B}}_{\mathsf{s}}({\bm{\beta}}_{*})\right\}.\end{split}

The proof will follow a similar approach as in
Misiakiewicz & Saeed 2024
;
Defilippis et al. 2024
and we will omit repetitive details. All inequality between random variables are understood to hold with probability at least
1-n^{-D}
for a fixed arbitrary
D>0
. We will allow the universal constants
C_{0},C_{1}
(appearing in
{\mathcal{E}}_{n_{\mathsf{s}},p_{\mathsf{s}},n_{\mathsf{t}},p_{\mathsf{t}}}
), and the constant
C_{*}
to change from line to line (in particular,
C_{*}
depends on
D
).

A.1
Deterministic approximation conditioned on the teacher

Conditioning on the teacher’s weights
{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}},
we have

\begin{split}\mathcal{R}({\bm{G}}_{\mathsf{s}},{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}},\lambda_{\mathsf{s}};{\bm{\beta}}_{*},{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}})=\|{\bm{\beta}}_{*}-p_{\mathsf{s}}^{-1/2}{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Xi}_{\mathsf{s}}{\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\bm{G}}_{\mathsf{s}}{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}}\|_{2}^{2}=\|{\bm{\beta}}_{*}-{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}}\|_{2}^{2}+\|{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}}-p_{\mathsf{s}}^{-1/2}{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Xi}_{\mathsf{s}}{\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\bm{G}}_{\mathsf{s}}{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}}\|_{2}^{2}\\
+2\langle{\bm{\beta}}_{*}-{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}},{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}}-p_{\mathsf{s}}^{-1/2}{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Xi}_{\mathsf{s}}{\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\bm{G}}_{\mathsf{s}}{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}}\rangle.\end{split}

The first term is deterministic after conditioning on the teacher.
The deterministic equivalent of the second term is known from
Misiakiewicz & Saeed 2024
;
Defilippis et al. 2024
, and we have

\begin{split}|\|{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}}-p_{\mathsf{s}}^{-1/2}{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Xi}_{\mathsf{s}}{\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\bm{G}}_{\mathsf{s}}{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}}\|_{2}^{2}-{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\top}{\bm{\Lambda}}_{\mathsf{t}}{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}}|\leq C_{*}{\mathcal{E}}_{n_{\mathsf{s}},p_{\mathsf{s}}}\frac{\mu_{\mathsf{s},2}{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\top}({\bm{\Sigma}}+\mu_{\mathsf{s},2})^{-1}{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}}}{1-\Upsilon_{n,p}(\mu_{\mathsf{s},1},\mu_{\mathsf{s},2})},\end{split}

where

\begin{split}{\bm{\Lambda}}_{\mathsf{t}}=\frac{\mu_{\mathsf{s},2}^{2}}{1-\Upsilon_{n,p}(\mu_{\mathsf{s},1},\mu_{\mathsf{s},2})}\left[({\bm{\Sigma}}+\mu_{\mathsf{s},2})^{-2}+\chi_{n,p}(\mu_{\mathsf{s},2}){\bm{\Sigma}}({\bm{\Sigma}}+\mu_{\mathsf{s},2})^{-2}\right].\end{split}

We note that the above bound is non-asymptotic compared to
(
Defilippis et al. 2024
, Theorem B.12)
, since we do not require that Assumption
2
holds for
{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}}.
The difference is that in
(
Defilippis et al. 2024
, Proof of Lemma B.13, step 3)
, we upper bound

\mu_{\mathsf{s},2}{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\top}({\bm{\Sigma}}+\mu_{\mathsf{s},2})^{-1}{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}}+(p_{\mathsf{s}}\mu_{\mathsf{s},1})\frac{{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\top}{\bm{\Sigma}}({\bm{\Sigma}}+\mu_{\mathsf{s},2})^{-2}{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}}}{p_{\mathsf{s}}-{\rm Tr}({\bm{\Sigma}}^{2}({\bm{\Sigma}}+\mu_{\mathsf{s},2})^{-2})},

by
\mu_{\mathsf{s},2}{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\top}({\bm{\Sigma}}+\mu_{\mathsf{s},2})^{-1}{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}}
instead of
{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\top}{\bm{\Lambda}}_{\mathsf{t}}{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}}
due to the missing Assumption
2
on
{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}}.
The rest follows the same argument as in
(
Defilippis et al. 2024
, Proof of Theorem B.12)
.

It remains to bound the cross term, and we first decompose

\begin{split}{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}}-p_{\mathsf{s}}^{-1/2}{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Xi}_{\mathsf{s}}{\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\bm{G}}_{\mathsf{s}}{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}}={\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{p_{\mathsf{s}}}}{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\top}({\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\top}{\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{s}}+\lambda_{\mathsf{s}})^{-1}{\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\top}{\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{s}}{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t},{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}}}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{p_{\mathsf{s}}}}{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\top}({\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\top}{\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{s}}+\lambda_{\mathsf{s}})^{-1}{\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\top}{\bm{R}}_{\mathsf{s}}{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t},{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}},\perp},\end{split}

and compute the deterministic equivalent w.r.t.
{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}}
. Here we introduced

{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t},{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}}}=\sqrt{p_{\mathsf{s}}}{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}}({\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\top}{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}})^{\dagger}{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}},\qquad{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t},{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}},\perp}={\mathsf{P}}_{\perp,{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}}}{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}},\qquad{\mathsf{P}}_{\perp,{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}}}={\bm{I}}-({\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\top}{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}})({\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\top}{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}})^{\dagger}.

We will also use
{\mathsf{P}}_{{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}}}=({\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\top}{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}})({\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\top}{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}})^{\dagger}
to denote the projection onto the span of
{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}}
.

For the deterministic equivalent of
\langle{\bm{\beta}}_{*}-{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}},\frac{1}{\sqrt{p_{\mathsf{s}}}}{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\top}({\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\top}{\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{s}}+\lambda_{\mathsf{s}})^{-1}{\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\top}{\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{s}}{\bm{\beta}}_{t,{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}}}\rangle,
we note that

\begin{split}&\langle{\bm{\beta}}_{*}-{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}},\frac{1}{\sqrt{p_{\mathsf{s}}}}{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\top}({\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\top}{\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{s}}+\lambda_{\mathsf{s}})^{-1}{\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\top}{\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{s}}{\bm{\beta}}_{t,{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}}}\rangle=\Phi_{2}({\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{s}};{\bm{v}}{\bm{u}}^{\top},\lambda_{\mathsf{s}}),\end{split}

where

\begin{split}{\bm{u}}=\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}}}^{1/2}{\bm{\beta}}_{t,{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}}},\quad{\bm{v}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{p_{\mathsf{s}}}}\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}}}^{-1/2}{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\top}({\bm{\beta}}_{*}-{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}}).\end{split}

By Theorem
6
, we have

\begin{split}|\Phi_{2}({\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{s}};{\bm{v}}{\bm{u}}^{\top},\lambda_{\mathsf{s}})-\Psi_{1}(\mu_{\mathsf{s},1};{\bm{v}}{\bm{u}}^{\top})|\leq C_{*}{\mathcal{E}}_{n_{\mathsf{s}},p_{\mathsf{s}}}\tilde{\Psi}_{1}(\mu_{\mathsf{s},1};{\bm{v}}{\bm{u}}^{\top}),\end{split}

where

\begin{split}&\Psi_{1}(\mu_{\mathsf{s},1};{\bm{v}}{\bm{u}}^{\top})=\langle{\bm{\beta}}_{*}-{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}},\frac{1}{\sqrt{p_{\mathsf{s}}}}{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\top}(\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{\mathsf{s}}+\mu_{\mathsf{s},1})^{-1}\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{\mathsf{s}}{\bm{\beta}}_{t,{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}}}\rangle,\\
&\tilde{\Psi}_{1}(\mu_{\mathsf{s},1};{\bm{v}}{\bm{u}}^{\top})=\left\{\|{\bm{u}}\|_{2}\sqrt{{\bm{v}}^{\top}(\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{\mathsf{s}}+\mu_{\mathsf{s},1})^{-2}\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{\mathsf{s}}^{2}{\bm{v}}}\right\}\vee\sqrt{{\bm{v}}^{\top}(\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{\mathsf{s}}+\mu_{\mathsf{s},1})^{-1}\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{\mathsf{s}}{\bm{v}}{\bm{u}}^{\top}({\bm{\Sigma}}_{\mathsf{s}}+\mu_{\mathsf{s},1})^{-1}{\bm{\Sigma}}_{\mathsf{s}}{\bm{u}}}.\end{split}

For the determinisic equivalent of
\langle{\bm{\beta}}_{*}-{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}},\frac{1}{\sqrt{p_{\mathsf{s}}}}{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\top}({\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\top}{\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{s}}+\lambda_{\mathsf{s}})^{-1}{\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\top}{\bm{R}}_{\mathsf{s}}{\bm{\beta}}_{t,{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}},\perp}\rangle,
by Lemma
1
we have:

\begin{split}|\langle{\bm{\beta}}_{*}-{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}},\frac{1}{\sqrt{p_{\mathsf{s}}}}{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\top}({\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\top}{\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{s}}+\lambda_{\mathsf{s}})^{-1}{\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\top}{\bm{R}}_{\mathsf{s}}{\bm{\beta}}_{t,{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}},\perp}\rangle|\leq C_{*}{\mathcal{E}}_{n_{\mathsf{s}}}\|{\bm{\beta}}_{t,{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}},\perp}\|_{2}\|{\bm{\beta}}_{*}-{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}}\|_{2},\end{split}

with

\begin{split}n_{\mathsf{s}}^{2}\Psi_{2}(\mu_{\mathsf{s},1};{\bm{v}}{\bm{v}}^{\top};{\bm{I}})=\frac{{\bm{v}}^{\top}(\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{\mathsf{s}}+\mu_{\mathsf{s},1})^{-2}\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{\mathsf{s}}^{2}{\bm{v}}}{1-\frac{1}{n_{\mathsf{s}}}{\rm Tr}({\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\top}{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}}({\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\top}{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}}+p_{\mathsf{s}}\mu_{\mathsf{s},1})^{-1})}.\end{split}

Note that

\begin{split}\langle{\bm{\beta}}_{*}-{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}},&\frac{1}{\sqrt{p_{\mathsf{s}}}}{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\top}(\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{\mathsf{s}}+\mu_{\mathsf{s},1})^{-1}\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{\mathsf{s}}{\bm{\beta}}_{t,{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}}}\rangle\\
=&\langle{\bm{\beta}}_{*}-{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}},{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\top}{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}}({\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\top}{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}}+p_{\mathsf{s}}\mu_{\mathsf{s},1})^{-1}{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\top}{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}}({\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\top}{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}})^{\dagger}{\bm{\beta}}_{t}\rangle\\
=&\langle{\bm{\beta}}_{*}-{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}},{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\top}{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}}({\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\top}{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}}+p_{\mathsf{s}}\mu_{\mathsf{s},1})^{-1}({\bm{I}}-\mathtt{P}_{\perp,{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}}}){\bm{\beta}}_{t}\rangle\\
=&\langle{\bm{\beta}}_{*}-{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}},{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\top}{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}}({\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\top}{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}}+p_{\mathsf{s}}\mu_{\mathsf{s},1})^{-1}{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}}\rangle,\end{split}

which implies that

\begin{split}\langle{\bm{\beta}}_{*}-{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}},{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}}\rangle&-\langle{\bm{\beta}}_{*}-{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}},\frac{1}{\sqrt{p_{\mathsf{s}}}}{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\top}(\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{\mathsf{s}}+\mu_{\mathsf{s},1})^{-1}\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_{\mathsf{s}}{\bm{\beta}}_{t,{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}}}\rangle\\
=&(p_{\mathsf{s}}\mu_{\mathsf{s}})\langle{\bm{\beta}}_{*}-{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}},({\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\top}{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}}+p_{\mathsf{s}}\mu_{\mathsf{s},1})^{-1}{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}}\rangle,\end{split}

and by Theorem
6
, we have

\begin{split}|(p_{\mathsf{s}}\mu_{\mathsf{s}})&\langle{\bm{\beta}}_{*}-{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}},({\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\top}{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}}+p_{\mathsf{s}}\mu_{\mathsf{s},1})^{-1}{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}}\rangle-\mu_{\mathsf{s},2}\langle{\bm{\beta}}_{*}-{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}},({\bm{\Sigma}}+\mu_{\mathsf{s},2})^{-1}{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}}\rangle|\\
\leq&C_{*}{\mathcal{E}}_{n_{\mathsf{s}},p_{\mathsf{s}}}\mu_{\mathsf{s},2}\sqrt{\langle{\bm{\beta}}_{*}-{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}},({\bm{\Sigma}}+\mu_{\mathsf{s},2})^{-1}({\bm{\beta}}_{*}-{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}})\rangle\langle{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}},({\bm{\Sigma}}+\mu_{\mathsf{s},2})^{-1}{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}}\rangle}.\end{split}

By combining the terms, we obtain:

\begin{split}|\|{\bm{\beta}}_{*}-p_{\mathsf{s}}^{-1/2}{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\Xi}_{\mathsf{s}}{\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\bm{G}}_{\mathsf{s}}{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}}\|_{2}^{2}-\mathcal{B}_{\mathsf{s}}({\bm{\beta}}_{*},{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}})|\leq\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathsf{s}}({\bm{\beta}}_{*},{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}}),\end{split}

where

\begin{split}\mathcal{B}_{\mathsf{s}}({\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}})&=\|{\bm{\beta}}_{*}-{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}}\|_{2}^{2}+2\mu_{\mathsf{s},2}\langle{\bm{\beta}}_{*}-{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}},({\bm{\Sigma}}+\mu_{\mathsf{s},2})^{-1}{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}}\rangle+{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\top}{\bm{\Lambda}}_{\mathsf{t}}{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}}\\
&=\|{\bm{\beta}}_{*}-{\bm{\Sigma}}({\bm{\Sigma}}+\mu_{\mathsf{s},2})^{-1}{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}}\|_{2}^{2}+{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\top}\overline{{\bm{\Lambda}}}_{0}{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}},\end{split}

and

\begin{split}\overline{{\bm{\Lambda}}}_{0}=\frac{\Upsilon_{n_{\mathsf{s}},p_{\mathsf{s}}}(\mu_{\mathsf{s},1},\mu_{\mathsf{s},2})}{1-\Upsilon_{n,p}(\mu_{\mathsf{s},1},\mu_{\mathsf{s},2})}\mu_{\mathsf{s},2}^{2}({\bm{\Sigma}}+\mu_{\mathsf{s},2})^{-2}+\frac{\chi_{n,p}(\mu_{\mathsf{s},2})}{1-\Upsilon_{n,p}(\mu_{\mathsf{s},1},\mu_{\mathsf{s},2})}\mu_{\mathsf{s},2}^{2}{\bm{\Sigma}}({\bm{\Sigma}}+\mu_{\mathsf{s},2})^{-2}.\end{split}

Finally, we compute the deterministic equivalent of the upper bounds. We note that each term in the approximation functional is at most
\|{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}}\|_{2}\|{\bm{\beta}}_{*}-{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}}\|_{2},
thus we have

\begin{split}\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathsf{s}}({\bm{\beta}}_{*},{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}})\leq\|{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}}\|_{2}\|{\bm{\beta}}_{*}-{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}}\|_{2}.\end{split}

A.2
Deterministic equivalent on the teacher’s model

Next, we compute the deterministic equivalent of
\mathcal{B}_{\mathsf{s}}({\bm{\beta}}_{*},{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}}).
We first decompose
\mathcal{B}_{\mathsf{s}}({\bm{\beta}}_{*},{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}})
as

\begin{split}\mathcal{B}_{\mathsf{s}}({\bm{\beta}}_{*},{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}})&=\|{\bm{\beta}}_{*}-{\bm{\Sigma}}({\bm{\Sigma}}+\mu_{\mathsf{s},2})^{-1}{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}}\|_{2}^{2}+{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\top}{\bm{\Lambda}}_{0}{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}}\\
&=\|{\bm{\beta}}_{*}\|_{2}^{2}-2\langle{\bm{\beta}}_{*},{\bm{\Sigma}}({\bm{\Sigma}}+\mu_{\mathsf{s},2})^{-1}{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}}\rangle+\langle{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}},{\bm{\Lambda}}_{0}{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}}\rangle,\end{split}

where

\begin{split}{\bm{\Lambda}}_{0}={\bm{\Sigma}}^{2}({\bm{\Sigma}}+\mu_{\mathsf{s},2})^{-2}+\frac{\Upsilon_{n_{\mathsf{s}},p_{\mathsf{s}}}(\mu_{\mathsf{s},1},\mu_{\mathsf{s},2})}{1-\Upsilon_{n,p}(\mu_{\mathsf{s},1},\mu_{\mathsf{s},2})}\mu_{\mathsf{s},2}^{2}({\bm{\Sigma}}+\mu_{\mathsf{s},2})^{-2}+\frac{\chi_{n,p}(\mu_{\mathsf{s},2})}{1-\Upsilon_{n,p}(\mu_{\mathsf{s},1},\mu_{\mathsf{s},2})}\mu_{\mathsf{s},2}^{2}{\bm{\Sigma}}({\bm{\Sigma}}+\mu_{\mathsf{s},2})^{-2}.\end{split}

We split
{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}}
as

\begin{split}{\bm{\beta}}_{\mathsf{t}}=&\frac{1}{\sqrt{p_{\mathsf{t}}}}{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\top}({\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\top}{\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{t}}+\lambda_{\mathsf{t}})^{-1}{\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\top}{\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{t}}{\bm{\beta}}_{{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{t}}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{p_{\mathsf{t}}}}{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\top}({\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\top}{\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{t}}+\lambda_{\mathsf{t}})^{-1}{\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\top}{\bm{R}}_{\mathsf{t}}{\bm{\beta}}_{\perp,{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{t}}}\\
&+\frac{1}{\sqrt{p_{\mathsf{t}}}}{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\top}({\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\top}{\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{t}}+\lambda_{\mathsf{t}})^{-1}{\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\top}{\bm{\varepsilon}}.\end{split}

We decompose the test error into the terms that depend on the teacher variance (i.e., terms that depend on
\varepsilon
), and terms that depend on the teacher bias (i.e., terms that do not depends on
\varepsilon
). We define

\begin{split}\mathcal{B}_{\mathsf{s},bias}({\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{t}})=&{\bm{\beta}}_{{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{t}}}^{\top}({\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\top}{\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{t}})({\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\top}{\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{t}}+\lambda_{\mathsf{t}})^{-1}\frac{{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{t}}{\bm{\Lambda}}_{0}{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\top}}{p_{\mathsf{t}}}({\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\top}{\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{t}}+\lambda_{\mathsf{t}})^{-1}({\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\top}{\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{t}}){\bm{\beta}}_{{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{t}}}\\
&+{\bm{\beta}}_{\perp,\mathsf{t}}^{\top}{\bm{R}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\top}{\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{t}}({\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\top}{\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{t}}+\lambda_{\mathsf{t}})^{-1}\frac{{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{t}}{\bm{\Lambda}}_{0}{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\top}}{p_{\mathsf{t}}}({\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\top}{\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{t}}+\lambda_{\mathsf{t}})^{-1}{\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\top}{\bm{R}}_{\mathsf{t}}{\bm{\beta}}_{\perp,\mathsf{t}}\\
&+{\bm{\beta}}_{\perp,\mathsf{t}}^{\top}{\bm{R}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\top}{\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{t}}({\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\top}{\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{t}}+\lambda_{\mathsf{t}})^{-1}\frac{{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{t}}{\bm{\Lambda}}_{0}{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\top}}{p_{\mathsf{t}}}({\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\top}{\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{t}}+\lambda_{\mathsf{t}})^{-1}({\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\top}{\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{t}}){\bm{\beta}}_{{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{t}}}\\
&+\frac{1}{\sqrt{p_{\mathsf{t}}}}\langle{\bm{\beta}}_{*},{\bm{\Lambda}}_{1}{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\top}({\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\top}{\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{t}}+\lambda_{\mathsf{t}})^{-1}{\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\top}{\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{t}}{\bm{\beta}}_{{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{t}}}\rangle\\
&+\frac{1}{\sqrt{p_{\mathsf{t}}}}\langle{\bm{\beta}}_{*},{\bm{\Lambda}}_{1}{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\top}({\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\top}{\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{t}}+\lambda_{\mathsf{t}})^{-1}{\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\top}{\bm{R}}_{\mathsf{t}}{\bm{\beta}}_{\perp,{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{t}}}\rangle,\\
\mathcal{B}_{\mathsf{s},var}({\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{t}})=&\tau_{\mathsf{t}}^{2}\frac{1}{p_{\mathsf{t}}}{\rm Tr}({\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{t}}{\bm{\Lambda}}_{0}{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\top}({\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\top}{\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{t}}+\lambda_{\mathsf{t}})^{-1}{\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\top}{\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{t}}({\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\top}{\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{t}}+\lambda_{\mathsf{t}})^{-1}).\end{split}

A.2.1
Terms depending on the teacher bias

In this part, we show the deterministic equivalent of
\mathcal{B}_{\mathsf{s},bias}({\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{t}}).

Quadratic term.

Let us consider the following quadratic term:

\begin{split}&{\bm{\beta}}_{{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{t}}}^{\top}({\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\top}{\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{t}})({\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\top}{\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{t}}+\lambda_{\mathsf{t}})^{-1}\frac{{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{t}}{\bm{\Lambda}}_{0}{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\top}}{p_{\mathsf{t}}}({\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\top}{\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{t}}+\lambda_{\mathsf{t}})^{-1}({\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\top}{\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{t}}){\bm{\beta}}_{{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{t}}}\\
&+{\bm{\beta}}_{\perp,\mathsf{t}}^{\top}{\bm{R}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\top}{\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{t}}({\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\top}{\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{t}}+\lambda_{\mathsf{t}})^{-1}\frac{{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{t}}{\bm{\Lambda}}_{0}{\bm{F}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\top}}{p_{\mathsf{t}}}({\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\top}{\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{t}}+\lambda_{\mathsf{t}})^{-1}{\bm{Z}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\top}{\bm{R}}_{\mathsf{t}}{\bm{\beta}}_{\perp,\mathsf{t}}\\
